[

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Schizophrenia, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHello, welcome in. I'm Dr. Smith. To start, can you tell me what's been bothering you or what brings you in today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 56 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can’t shake the feeling that certain staff emails and morning announcements at school are really coded messages about me, even when my colleagues gently show me they were sent to everyone.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… thank you, Doctor. I’m here because something at work has been really bothering me, and I know it may sound a bit hard to explain. I’m a teacher, and lately I can’t seem to shake this feeling that certain staff emails, and even some of the morning announcements at school, are… not exactly what they appear to be.

I mean, they’re sent out to everyone, I know that. My colleagues have shown me that—quite gently, actually—and they’ll say, “See, this went to the whole staff,” or “That announcement was just about the schedule.” And I can see that on the screen. I can understand it logically. But still, there’s this strong feeling that there’s something coded in them, like they’re really referring to me, or about me in some way.

It’s not every single email or announcement, but certain ones just catch at me. A phrase will stand out, or the timing of it, or the way something is worded, and then I start thinking, “Why did they say it like that?” It’s been distracting and upsetting, because I don’t want to be someone who overreacts, and I don’t want to accuse anyone. But the feeling doesn’t really go away just because someone tells me it’s ordinary. That’s what brought me in.
=====================================

It’s been months, not just a few days. I think… about four months now, if I’m being honest. At first I told myself it was just stress or that I was over-reading things, because teachers get so many emails and announcements that it’s easy for something to feel pointed. But it didn’t really pass.

  {

I'm Dr. Smith. To start, can you tell me what's been bothering you or what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… thank you, Doctor. I’m here because something at work has been really bothering me, and I know it may sound a bit hard to explain. I’m a teacher, and lately I can’t seem to shake this feeling that certain staff emails, and even some of the morning announcements at school, are… not exactly what they appear to be.\n\nI mean, they’re sent out to everyone, I know that. My colleagues have shown me that—quite gently, actually—and they’ll say, “See, this went to the whole staff,” or “That announcement was just about the schedule.” And I can see that on the screen. I can understand it logically. But still, there’s this strong feeling that there’s something coded in them, like they’re really referring to me, or about me in some way.\n\nIt’s not every single email or announcement, but certain ones just catch at me. But the feeling doesn’t really go away just because someone tells me it’s ordinary. That’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing this feeling that certain emails or announcements are coded messages about you—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: It’s been months, not just a few days. I think… about four months now, if I’m being honest. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels more like I actually hear it, not just a thought. That’s the part that bothers me, because I know the difference between my own inner voice and, you know, thinking something to myself.

It usually happens when things are quiet—like when I’m at home and the house has settled, or after the classroom has emptied and there’s that stillness after all the students have gone. I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name, or make a very short comment about what I’m doing. Not a long conversation, not usually, just something brief. But it’s clear enough that I’ll answer, almost automatically, before I catch myself and realize there isn’t anyone there.

And then I feel foolish, because I’ll look around like someone must have come in, or I’ll think maybe it was from the hallway or outside, but there isn’t anyone. It doesn’t feel like I’m choosing to imagine it. It feels like it lands on my ears, if that makes sense. Then afterward I start questioning myself—was it really a sound, did I mishear something, am I just tired—but in the moment, yes, it seems like a voice outside of me.

I’m a teacher, and lately I can’t seem to shake this feeling that certain staff emails, and even some of the morning announcements at school, are… not exactly what they appear to be.\n\nI mean, they’re sent out to everyone, I know that. My colleagues have shown me that—quite gently, actually—and they’ll say, “See, this went to the whole staff,” or “That announcement was just about the schedule.” And I can see that on the screen. I can understand it logically. But still, there’s this strong feeling that there’s something coded in them, like they’re really referring to me, or about me in some way.\n\nIt’s not every single email or announcement, but certain ones just catch at me. A phrase will stand out, or the timing of it, or the way something is worded, and then I start thinking, “Why did they say it like that?” It’s been distracting and upsetting, because I don’t want to be someone who overreacts, and I don’t want to accuse anyone. But the feeling doesn’t really go away just because someone tells me it’s ordinary. That’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing this feeling that certain emails or announcements are coded messages about you—days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: It’s been months, not just a few days. I think… about four months now, if I’m being honest. At first I told myself it was just stress or that I was over-reading things, because teachers get so many emails and announcements that it’s easy for something to feel pointed. But it didn’t really pass.\n\nThe coded-message feeling has been there on and off for roughly four months, and around the same stretch of time there’s also been the voice I sometimes find myself answering. I know that’s probably important to say, even though it feels embarrassing. So yes—about four months.\n\nDoctor: When you say you sometimes find yourself answering a voice, can you describe that experience—do you actually hear it as a sound coming from outside you, like someone speaking in the room, or is it more like a thought in your head?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I actually hear it, not just a thought. That’s the part that bothers me, because I know the difference between my own inner voice and, you know, thinking something to myself.\n\nIt usually happens when things are quiet—like when I’m at home and the house has settled, or after the classroom has emptied and there’s that stillness after all the students have gone. I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name, or make a very short comment about what I’m doing. Not a long conversation, not usually, just something brief. But it’s clear enough that I’ll answer, almost automatically, before I catch myself and realize there isn’t anyone there.\n\nAnd then I feel foolish, because I’ll look around like someone must have come in, or I’ll think maybe it was from the hallway or outside, but there isn’t anyone. It doesn’t feel like I’m choosing to imagine it. It feels like it lands on my ears, if that makes sense. Then afterward I start questioning myself—was it really a sound, did I mishear something, am I just tired—but in the moment, yes, it seems like a voice outside of me.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver these past four months, have you noticed any changes in how well you're able to do your job or keep up with your usual routines and relationships—for example, trouble concentrating in class, keeping up with grading, or connecting with friends and family—apart from the specific worries about the messages and the voice?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose there have been changes, though I’ve been trying very hard to keep things looking normal. I’m still teaching. I haven’t stopped going in, and I’m not missing whole days or anything like that. But I’m not functioning quite the way I usually do, if I’m being honest.

The biggest place I notice it is with project lessons. Those are usually something I can manage fairly well—moving students into groups, keeping track of materials, circulating, answering questions, adjusting when something goes off track. Lately, though, it feels harder to hold all the moving pieces in my head. A couple of times I’ve had coworkers step in and cover parts of those lessons for me, not the entire class, but enough that I didn’t have to manage every part myself. I felt embarrassed about that, because I’ve been teaching a long time and I’m used to being the one who has it together.

Planning meetings have been difficult too. I still attend them, but I don’t always get through them smoothly. Sometimes a colleague will help me keep track of what was decided, or sort of guide me back to the agenda. I’ve needed more help than I like to admit.

And outside of work… yes, I’ve pulled back. I used to be reliable for things. Book club, going to the theater with friends, neighborhood events—if I said I’d be there, I was there. Now I either cancel, or I don’t commit, or I say I’ll come and then I just can’t make myself follow through. People have been kind, but I know I’ve stopped being dependable in that way. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I just feel less able to keep up with everything.

I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name, or make a very short comment about what I’m doing. Not a long conversation, not usually, just something brief. But it’s clear enough that I’ll answer, almost automatically, before I catch myself and realize there isn’t anyone there.\n\nAnd then I feel foolish, because I’ll look around like someone must have come in, or I’ll think maybe it was from the hallway or outside, but there isn’t anyone. It doesn’t feel like I’m choosing to imagine it. It feels like it lands on my ears, if that makes sense. Then afterward I start questioning myself—was it really a sound, did I mishear something, am I just tired—but in the moment, yes, it seems like a voice outside of me.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months, have you noticed any changes in how well you're able to do your job or keep up with your usual routines and relationships—for example, trouble concentrating in class, keeping up with grading, or connecting with friends and family—apart from the specific worries about the messages and the voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose there have been changes, though I’ve been trying very hard to keep things looking normal. I’m still teaching. I haven’t stopped going in, and I’m not missing whole days or anything like that. But I’m not functioning quite the way I usually do, if I’m being honest.\n\nThe biggest place I notice it is with project lessons. Those are usually something I can manage fairly well—moving students into groups, keeping track of materials, circulating, answering questions, adjusting when something goes off track. Lately, though, it feels harder to hold all the moving pieces in my head. A couple of times I’ve had coworkers step in and cover parts of those lessons for me, not the entire class, but enough that I didn’t have to manage every part myself. I felt embarrassed about that, because I’ve been teaching a long time and I’m used to being the one who has it together.\n\nPlanning meetings have been difficult too. I still attend them, but I don’t always get through them smoothly. Sometimes a colleague will help me keep track of what was decided, or sort of guide me back to the agenda. I’ve needed more help than I like to admit.\n\nAnd outside of work… yes, I’ve pulled back. I used to be reliable for things. Book club, going to the theater with friends, neighborhood events—if I said I’d be there, I was there. Now I either cancel, or I don’t commit, or I say I’ll come and then I just can’t make myself follow through. People have been kind, but I know I’ve stopped being dependable in that way. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I just feel less able to keep up with everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver these past four months, have you also noticed changes in your mood—like feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or unusually elevated or irritable—for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 5\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I’ve been upset and worn down by all of this, obviously, and embarrassed at times, but I don’t think I’ve been sad all day, every day, or hopeless in that constant sort of way you’re asking about.

So, I suppose the honest answer is that my mood hasn’t felt like the main change. I’ve been worried and stressed, yes, but not persistently depressed or unusually elevated in the way you mean.

sound coming from outside you, like someone speaking in the room, or is it more like a thought in your head?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I actually hear it, not just a thought. That’s the part that bothers me, because I know the difference between my own inner voice and, you know, thinking something to myself.\n\nIt usually happens when things are quiet—like when I’m at home and the house has settled, or after the classroom has emptied and there’s that stillness after all the students have gone. I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name, or make a very short comment about what I’m doing. Not a long conversation, not usually, just something brief. But it’s clear enough that I’ll answer, almost automatically, before I catch myself and realize there isn’t anyone there.\n\nAnd then I feel foolish, because I’ll look around like someone must have come in, or I’ll think maybe it was from the hallway or outside, but there isn’t anyone. It doesn’t feel like I’m choosing to imagine it. It feels like it lands on my ears, if that makes sense. Then afterward I start questioning myself—was it really a sound, did I mishear something, am I just tired—but in the moment, yes, it seems like a voice outside of me.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months, have you noticed any changes in how well you're able to do your job or keep up with your usual routines and relationships—for example, trouble concentrating in class, keeping up with grading, or connecting with friends and family—apart from the specific worries about the messages and the voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose there have been changes, though I’ve been trying very hard to keep things looking normal. I’m still teaching. I haven’t stopped going in, and I’m not missing whole days or anything like that. But I’m not functioning quite the way I usually do, if I’m being honest.\n\nThe biggest place I notice it is with project lessons. Those are usually something I can manage fairly well—moving students into groups, keeping track of materials, circulating, answering questions, adjusting when something goes off track. Lately, though, it feels harder to hold all the moving pieces in my head. A couple of times I’ve had coworkers step in and cover parts of those lessons for me, not the entire class, but enough that I didn’t have to manage every part myself. I felt embarrassed about that, because I’ve been teaching a long time and I’m used to being the one who has it together.\n\nPlanning meetings have been difficult too. I still attend them, but I don’t always get through them smoothly. Sometimes a colleague will help me keep track of what was decided, or sort of guide me back to the agenda. I’ve needed more help than I like to admit.\n\nAnd outside of work… yes, I’ve pulled back. I used to be reliable for things. Book club, going to the theater with friends, neighborhood events—if I said I’d be there, I was there. Now I either cancel, or I don’t commit, or I say I’ll come and then I just can’t make myself follow through. People have been kind, but I know I’ve stopped being dependable in that way. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I just feel less able to keep up with everything.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months, have you also noticed changes in your mood—like feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or unusually elevated or irritable—for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I’ve been upset and worn down by all of this, obviously, and embarrassed at times, but I don’t think I’ve been sad all day, every day, or hopeless in that constant sort of way you’re asking about.\n\nAnd I wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt unusually up or energetic or like I didn’t need sleep or anything like that. Irritable… maybe a bit, but I think that’s more because I’m tense and second-guessing myself, not like a big change in my mood that takes over for days.\n\nSo, I suppose the honest answer is that my mood hasn’t felt like the main change. I’ve been worried and stressed, yes, but not persistently depressed or unusually elevated in the way you mean.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been using any alcohol, recreational drugs, or new medications, or have you had any recent medical illnesses, around the time these experiences started?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think there were earlier signs, though I didn’t really call them that at the time. The four months with the coded messages and the voice feel like the part that became impossible to ignore, but the broader change started before that.

It was around when my youngest left for college—so, about a year and two months ago now. I remember thinking I was just adjusting to the house being quieter, and to that whole shift in identity, I suppose. You spend so many years organizing yourself around school runs and meals and keeping track of everyone’s schedules, and then suddenly there’s this space. At first I thought feeling different was just normal loneliness or being a bit unmoored.

But looking back, that’s when I started feeling less like myself in a wider way. Not one dramatic thing, more like my thinking, my work rhythm, and how I connected with people all began to feel… altered. I was still doing things, still teaching, still seeing people here and there, but it felt like I was pushing through something I couldn’t quite name. I didn’t understand it as a warning sign then. I just kept telling myself I was adapting to my youngest being gone.

But it didn’t really pass.\n\nThe coded-message feeling has been there on and off for roughly four months, and around the same stretch of time there’s also been the voice I sometimes find myself answering. I know that’s probably important to say, even though it feels embarrassing. So yes—about four months.\n\nDoctor: When you say you sometimes find yourself answering a voice, can you describe that experience—do you actually hear it as a sound coming from outside you, like someone speaking in the room, or is it more like a thought in your head?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I actually hear it, not just a thought. That’s the part that bothers me, because I know the difference between my own inner voice and, you know, thinking something to myself.\n\nIt usually happens when things are quiet—like when I’m at home and the house has settled, or after the classroom has emptied and there’s that stillness after all the students have gone. I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name, or make a very short comment about what I’m doing. Not a long conversation, not usually, just something brief. But it’s clear enough that I’ll answer, almost automatically, before I catch myself and realize there isn’t anyone there.\n\nAnd then I feel foolish, because I’ll look around like someone must have come in, or I’ll think maybe it was from the hallway or outside, but there isn’t anyone. It doesn’t feel like I’m choosing to imagine it. It feels like it lands on my ears, if that makes sense. Then afterward I start questioning myself—was it really a sound, did I mishear something, am I just tired—but in the moment, yes, it seems like a voice outside of me.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months, have you noticed any changes in how well you're able to do your job or keep up with your usual routines and relationships—for example, trouble concentrating in class, keeping up with grading, or connecting with friends and family—apart from the specific worries about the messages and the voice?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose there have been changes, though I’ve been trying very hard to keep things looking normal. I’m still teaching. I haven’t stopped going in, and I’m not missing whole days or anything like that. But I’m not functioning quite the way I usually do, if I’m being honest.\n\nThe biggest place I notice it is with project lessons. Those are usually something I can manage fairly well—moving students into groups, keeping track of materials, circulating, answering questions, adjusting when something goes off track. Lately, though, it feels harder to hold all the moving pieces in my head. A couple of times I’ve had coworkers step in and cover parts of those lessons for me, not the entire class, but enough that I didn’t have to manage every part myself. I felt embarrassed about that, because I’ve been teaching a long time and I’m used to being the one who has it together.\n\nPlanning meetings have been difficult too. I still attend them, but I don’t always get through them smoothly. Sometimes a colleague will help me keep track of what was decided, or sort of guide me back to the agenda. I’ve needed more help than I like to admit.\n\nAnd outside of work… yes, I’ve pulled back. I used to be reliable for things. Book club, going to the theater with friends, neighborhood events—if I said I’d be there, I was there. Now I either cancel, or I don’t commit, or I say I’ll come and then I just can’t make myself follow through. People have been kind, but I know I’ve stopped being dependable in that way. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I just feel less able to keep up with everything.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months, have you also noticed changes in your mood—like feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or unusually elevated or irritable—for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I’ve been upset and worn down by all of this, obviously, and embarrassed at times, but I don’t think I’ve been sad all day, every day, or hopeless in that constant sort of way you’re asking about.\n\nAnd I wouldn’t say I’ve had periods where I felt unusually up or energetic or like I didn’t need sleep or anything like that. Irritable… maybe a bit, but I think that’s more because I’m tense and second-guessing myself, not like a big change in my mood that takes over for days.\n\nSo, I suppose the honest answer is that my mood hasn’t felt like the main change. I’ve been worried and stressed, yes, but not persistently depressed or unusually elevated in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any alcohol, recreational drugs, or new medications, or have you had any recent medical illnesses, around the time these experiences started?\n\nPatient: I don’t really have a clear answer for that, I’m afraid. I’ve been trying to think back over it, because it seems like the kind of thing that should have an obvious starting point, but nothing stands out to me.\n\nI haven’t noticed anything in particular around when it began that I can point to and say, “Yes, that was different.” I mean, nothing that feels obviously connected. I’m not aware of some big change or event medically, and I’m not thinking of anything I started taking right then that would explain it. But I also don’t want to say that too confidently, because I don’t always remember the timing of little things perfectly.\n\nAs for alcohol or drugs… I’m not someone who uses recreational drugs. Alcohol isn’t a major part of my life. I might have a drink socially now and then, but I haven’t noticed it lining up with these experiences or anything like that.\n\nSo… no, not that I can tell. I wish I had something more concrete to give you, but I really can’t think of a substance or new medication or illness that seemed to come before this.\n\nDoctor: Before these four months when the coded messages and voice started, did you notice any earlier, milder signs—like feeling unusually suspicious, withdrawing from people, or just feeling 'off' in a way that was different from your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there were earlier signs, though I didn’t really call them that at the time. The four months with the coded messages and the voice feel like the part that became impossible to ignore, but the broader change started before that.\n\nIt was around when my youngest left for college—so, about a year and two months ago now. I remember thinking I was just adjusting to the house being quieter, and to that whole shift in identity, I suppose. You spend so many years organizing yourself around school runs and meals and keeping track of everyone’s schedules, and then suddenly there’s this space. At first I thought feeling different was just normal loneliness or being a bit unmoored.\n\nBut looking back, that’s when I started feeling less like myself in a wider way. Not one dramatic thing, more like my thinking, my work rhythm, and how I connected with people all began to feel… altered. I was still doing things, still teaching, still seeing people here and there, but it felt like I was pushing through something I couldn’t quite name. I didn’t understand it as a warning sign then. I just kept telling myself I was adapting to my youngest being gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think there were any real stretches where I felt completely back to my old self. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because some days were better than others, of course, and I could still go into work and have a normal conversation and get things done. So from the outside it probably looked as if I was carrying on.

But inside, that altered feeling was there pretty much continuously from when my youngest left for college, about a year and two months ago. It wasn’t always dramatic, and it wasn’t always something I would have complained about, but the way I was thinking, the way I worked, and the way I connected with people just didn’t feel quite normal again.

So before the coded messages and the voice started, I wouldn’t say I had gone back to baseline and then suddenly changed. It was more like this ongoing shift had been there all along, and then things became more obvious about four months ago.

I wish I had something more concrete to give you, but I really can’t think of a substance or new medication or illness that seemed to come before this.\n\nDoctor: Before these four months when the coded messages and voice started, did you notice any earlier, milder signs—like feeling unusually suspicious, withdrawing from people, or just feeling 'off' in a way that was different from your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there were earlier signs, though I didn’t really call them that at the time. The four months with the coded messages and the voice feel like the part that became impossible to ignore, but the broader change started before that.\n\nIt was around when my youngest left for college—so, about a year and two months ago now. I remember thinking I was just adjusting to the house being quieter, and to that whole shift in identity, I suppose. You spend so many years organizing yourself around school runs and meals and keeping track of everyone’s schedules, and then suddenly there’s this space. At first I thought feeling different was just normal loneliness or being a bit unmoored.\n\nBut looking back, that’s when I started feeling less like myself in a wider way. Not one dramatic thing, more like my thinking, my work rhythm, and how I connected with people all began to feel… altered. I was still doing things, still teaching, still seeing people here and there, but it felt like I was pushing through something I couldn’t quite name. I didn’t understand it as a warning sign then. I just kept telling myself I was adapting to my youngest being gone.\n\nDoctor: Since that time about fourteen months ago when you first noticed feeling 'off,' has that altered sense of yourself and your functioning been continuous, or were there stretches where you felt completely back to your normal self before the coded messages and voice started four months ago?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there were any real stretches where I felt completely back to my old self. That’s the part that’s hard to explain, because some days were better than others, of course, and I could still go into work and have a normal conversation and get things done. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. I’m here because something at work has been really bothering me, and I know it may sound a bit hard to explain. I’m a teacher, and lately I can’t seem to shake this feeling that certain staff emails, and even some of the morning announcements at school, are… not exactly what they appear to be.\n\nI mean, they’re sent out to everyone, I know that. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S020\",\"name\":\"Hallucinations\",\"description\":\"Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking whether the voice is heard externally like a real sound or internally like a thought, which directly concerns hallucinations.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nIt feels more like I actually hear it, not just a thought. That’s the part that bothers me, because I know the difference between my own inner voice and, you know, thinking something to myself.\n\nIt usually happens when things are quiet—like when I’m at home and the house has settled, or after the classroom has emptied and there’s that stillness after all the students have gone. I’ll hear a woman’s voice say my name, or make a very short comment about what I’m doing. Not a long conversation, not usually, just something brief. But it’s clear enough that I’ll answer, almost automatically, before I catch myself and realize there isn’t anyone there.\n\nAnd then I feel foolish, because I’ll look around like someone must have come in, or I’ll think maybe it was from the hallway or outside, but there isn’t anyone. It doesn’t feel like I’m choosing to imagine it. It feels like it lands on my ears, if that makes sense. Then afterward I start questioning myself—was it really a sound, did I mishear something, am I just tired—but in the moment, yes, it seems like a voice outside of me.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========
{"confirmed":["S020"],"denied":[],"reasoning":{"S020":"The patient describes clearly hearing a woman's voice say their name or make brief comments when no one is present, experienced as an external sound rather than an inner thought."}}

Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYes… I suppose there have been changes, though I’ve been trying very hard to keep things looking normal. I’m still teaching. I haven’t stopped going in, and I’m not missing whole days or anything like that. But I’m not functioning quite the way I usually do, if I’m being honest.\n\nThe biggest place I notice it is with project lessons. Those are usually something I can manage fairly well—moving students into groups, keeping track of materials, circulating, answering questions, adjusting when something goes off track. Lately, though, it feels harder to hold all the moving pieces in my head. A couple of times I’ve had coworkers step in and cover parts of those lessons for me, not the entire class, but enough that I didn’t have to manage every part myself. I felt embarrassed about that, because I’ve been teaching a long time and I’m used to being the one who has it together.\n\nPlanning meetings have been difficult too. I still attend them, but I don’t always get through them smoothly. Sometimes a colleague will help me keep track of what was decided, or sort of guide me back to the agenda. I’ve needed more help than I like to admit.\n\nAnd outside of work… yes, I’ve pulled back. I used to be reliable for things. Book club, going to the theater with friends, neighborhood events—if I said I’d be there, I was there. Now I either cancel, or I don’t commit, or I say I’ll come and then I just can’t make myself follow through. People have been kind, but I know I’ve stopped being dependable in that way. It’s not that I don’t care about them. I just feel less able to keep up with everything.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
